# Debt-track Disagreement Queue

**Module:** Debt Defense Prototype (all states/federal) - **Layer:** grounded-corroboration + adversarial-generation
**Runner rule:** `scripts/corroboration/run_corroboration.py` appends new flagged items only. It never edits or
overwrites the Resolution, Resolved by, or Date fields -- those belong to Andy / the certifying attorney.
Built per DEBT_PROJECT_ARCHITECTURE_SPEC.md S3(d), generalizing Direction D-2 (`docs/DIRECTION_D_ROADMAP.md`) into
the debt track, per the 2026-08-26 Phase-A-Unblock direction item 6.

> **How to use this queue:** work top-to-bottom. Each entry has a candidate classification (rule gap / model error /
> citation-aggregator error) computed mechanically by the runner as a *hint*, not a determination -- the runner does
> not and cannot decide which side is legally correct. Fill in Resolution and Resolved-by, then move to Resolved.

In fact 42 U.S.C. § 407, 38 U.S.C. § 5301, and the anti-garnishment rule at 31 C.F.R. pt. 212 (requiring banks to auto-protect two months of direct-deposited federal benefits) make these funds exempt and largely self-executing. The node would confidently apply the $100,000 Texas family cap and produce a materially wrong answer; separately, it never flags that a Texas bankruptcy debtor may elect the federal exemption scheme instead. **Per-model derivation results:**
  - claude-opus-5: grounded=True, error=None, summary='Under Tex. Prop. Code § 42.001(a), personal property of the types listed in § 42.002(a) — home furnishings, provisions, farm/ranch implements, trade tools (including boats and motor vehicles used in a trade), clothing, jewelry (capped at 25% of the aggregate limit), two firearms, athletic equipment, one motor vehicle per licensed family member or single adult, specified livestock and fowl, and household pets — is exempt from garnishment, attachment, execution, or other seizure up to an aggregate fair market value of $100,000 for a family or $50,000 for a single adult, excluding liens and other encumbrances. Section 42.001(b) exempts additional property outside that cap: current wages for personal services (except for court-ordered child support), professionally prescribed health aids, alimony/support/separate maintenance, and a bible or sacred writings (except when seized by a landlord exercising lease-related seizure rights). Further exemptions apply in addition to the cap for tax-qualified retirement and savings plans and 60-day/rollover distributions (§ 42.0021), Texas Tomorrow Fund and Section 529 college savings plans (§ 42.0022), and life insurance and annuity cash value and proceeds (Tex. Ins. Code § 1108.051). These exemptions do not apply to child support liens under Family Code Chapter 157, Subchapter G (§ 42.005), and property acquired or improved with non-exempt assets with intent to defraud, delay, or hinder creditors is not exempt, though a creditor must assert such a claim within two years of acquisition (§ 42.004).'
  - gpt-5.5: grounded=True, error=None, summary='Texas exempts certain personal property from garnishment, attachment, execution, or other seizure up to an aggregate fair market value of $100,000 for a family or $50,000 for a single adult who is not a member of a family, excluding liens and other encumbrances. The exempt property includes the listed categories in Section 42.002(a), while certain items—such as current wages, prescribed health aids, support payments, and certain sacred writings—are exempt outside those aggregate caps. Additional exemptions apply to qualifying retirement/savings plans, certain education savings plans, and insurance or annuity benefits, but the personal-property exemptions do not apply to child support liens and may be lost for property acquired with intent to defraud, delay, or hinder an interested person.'
  - gemini-2.5-pro: grounded=True, error=None, summary='Under Texas law, personal property is exempt from creditor seizure up to an aggregate fair market value of $100,000 for a family or $50,000 for a single adult. The property covered by this limit includes items such as home furnishings, tools of a trade, two firearms, and one motor vehicle per licensed driver. Certain other assets are fully exempt and do not count toward this dollar limit, including current wages, retirement accounts, life insurance proceeds, and prescribed health aids. These exemptions generally do not apply against court-ordered child support liens or if property was acquired to defraud a creditor.'

### [TX-HOMESTEAD-EXEMPTION] ADVERSARIAL-GAP -- run run_20260831T082700Z, 2026-08-31T08:58:19Z

**File:** `rules/debt/state/texas/tx_debt_state_layer_v1.json`
**Classification hint (mechanical, not authoritative):** ADVERSARIAL-GAP
**Evidence:** 3 adversarial edge case(s) flagged as exposing a gap: [{'scenario': 'A debtor moved from Illinois to Texas 16 months ago, bought a home with substantial equity, and now files Chapter 7 in Texas. He asks whether his home is protected, having heard Texas has unlimited homestead protection.', 'realistic_and_common': True, 'would_cause_wrong_answer': True, 'gap_description': "The node's bankruptcy caveat addresses only 11 U.S.C. § 522(p)'s 1,215-day equity cap, but omits § 522(b)(3)(A)'s 730-day domicile rule: a debtor domiciled in Texas for less than two years before filing must use the exemptions of the prior state of domicile (or the federal exemptions if the prior state's are unavailable to nonresidents). Such a debtor gets no Texas homestead exemption at all, not merely a $214,000 cap. Given Texas's very high in-migration, this is a common posture, and the encoded rule would tell the debtor he is protected up to $214,000 when he may in fact be limited to a prior state's much smaller exemption or the ~$31,575 federal figure.", 'exposes_gap': True}, {'scenario': 'A homeowner takes a two-year work assignment in another state, leases out her Texas house, and does not buy or claim a homestead elsewhere. A judgment creditor levies on the house, and she asks whether it is still her homestead.', 'realistic_and_common': True, 'would_cause_wrong_answer': True, 'gap_description': "The checklist makes 'whether the property is the debtor's actual residence' dispositive, with no treatment of Texas abandonment law. Under Tex. Const. art. XVI § 51 and settled case law, temporary renting or absence does not abandon a homestead absent an overt act plus intent to abandon, and generally not unless another homestead is acquired; conversely, a debtor who has acquired a homestead elsewhere loses it even if still nominally 'residing' part-time in Texas. As encoded, a temporarily absent owner would be told the exemption is lost when it is not — a materially wrong answer in a common relocation/rental fact pattern.", 'exposes_gap': True}, {'scenario': "A married couple hires a remodeling contractor who begins work on an oral understanding, later has only the husband sign a written contract and mechanic's lien, and now seeks to foreclose on the homestead after a payment dispute.", 'realistic_and_common': True, 'would_cause_wrong_answer': True, 'gap_description': "The exception is encoded simply as 'home-improvement liens contracted in writing,' omitting the constitutional formalities of Tex. Const. art. XVI § 50(a)(5) and Tex. Prop. Code § 53.254: the contract must be signed by both spouses, executed before any materials are delivered or labor performed, filed with the county clerk, and (for a residence) subject to the statutory cooling-off/right-of-rescission provisions. A lien failing these requirements is void against the homestead. As encoded, the homeowners would be told their homestead can be foreclosed when the lien is in fact unenforceable — and contractor payment disputes on homesteads are very common.", 'exposes_gap': True}]

**Per-model derivation results:**
  - claude-opus-5: grounded=True, error=None, summary="Under Tex. Prop. Code § 41.001(a), a Texas homestead (and burial lots) is exempt from seizure for creditors' claims, except for encumbrances properly fixed on the homestead property; the exemption is unlimited in value but limited by area under § 41.002 — up to 10 acres for an urban home, and for a rural home up to 200 acres for a family or 100 acres for a single adult, including improvements. In bankruptcy, 11 U.S.C. § 522(p) caps the exemptible homestead interest acquired within the 1,215 days before filing (statutory base $250,000; currently $214,000 for cases filed April 1, 2025–March 31, 2028), and § 522(o) reduces the exempt value attributable to property the debtor disposed of within 10 years before filing with intent to hinder, delay, or defraud a creditor. The state exemption does not defeat federal tax liens: under 26 U.S.C. § 6321 the lien attaches to all property of the delinquent taxpayer, and United States v. Rodgers, 461 U.S. 677 (1983), held that such a lien reaches homestead property under the Supremacy Clause and that a federal court may order its judicial sale."
  - gpt-5.5: grounded=True, error=None, summary='Texas law exempts a homestead, and burial lots, from seizure for creditor claims, except for encumbrances properly fixed on homestead property. The protected homestead is limited to up to 10 acres for an urban home, up to 200 acres for a rural family homestead, or up to 100 acres for a rural single-adult homestead. The excerpt also identifies federal limits or overrides: certain recently acquired bankruptcy homestead interests may be capped or reduced for fraudulent transfers, and federal tax liens can attach to and be enforced against homestead property despite the state exemption.'
  - gemini-2.5-pro: grounded=True, error=None, summary="Under Texas law, a homestead is exempt from seizure by creditors, except for properly fixed encumbrances. An urban homestead can be up to 10 acres, while a rural homestead can be up to 200 acres for a family. However, this exemption does not apply to federal tax liens, which can attach to the property. In bankruptcy, the exemption's value may also be capped for recently acquired property or reduced if it was funded with assets transferred to defraud creditors."

Eviction cases are governed by the Rule 510 series, which displaces inconsistent Part V rules: TRCP 510.8(e) bars a motion for new trial in an eviction case entirely, and the remedy is a de novo appeal to county court within 5 days of judgment (TRCP 510.9), with a statement of inability to pay available in lieu of bond. Advising this tenant she has 14 days to file a motion for new trial is affirmatively wrong on both the available remedy and the deadline, and by day 8 the actual 5-day appeal window has already closed (though a late-filed appeal bond/statement issue and possession-bond timing may still matter). Eviction defaults are among the most common default judgments in Texas.', 'exposes_gap': True}, {'scenario': 'A consumer defaulted in a justice court debt-buyer suit; he learns of the judgment on day 18 after it was signed (he did have notice within 20 days, so TRCP 306a does not help) and asks what he can do.', 'realistic_and_common': True, 'would_cause_wrong_answer': True, 'gap_description': 'The checklist treats the justice-court filing deadline as a dispositive gate that forecloses relief once missed, but omits the de novo appeal route: TRCP 506.1 allows appeal from a justice court judgment to county court within 21 days of the judgment (or of denial of a motion for new trial), with a sworn statement of inability to pay in place of a bond, and that appeal vacates the judgment and produces an entirely new trial without any Craddock showing. Telling this person the 14-day window closed the path is materially wrong -- he has a superior, non-discretionary remedy with three days left. This is a deterministic gate of exactly the kind the node otherwise commits to surfacing.', 'exposes_gap': True}, {'scenario': 'A defendant was served by citation by publication (a divorce, tax-foreclosure, or quiet-title style case) after the plaintiff filed an affidavit of diligent search; he learns of the default judgment six months later when it shows up on a title search, and service by publication was procedurally proper.', 'realistic_and_common': True, 'would_cause_wrong_answer': True, 'gap_description': "The node's escape hatches are TRCP 306a (max 90 days from signing) and the Peralta/defective-service path. The node omits TRCP 329, which gives a defendant served only by publication who did not appear a full two years after the judgment is signed to move for a new trial on a sworn showing of good defense. Applying the encoded rule would tell this person the motion-for-new-trial path is dead and steer him to a bill of review, when a much simpler and more favorable statutory remedy is squarely available.", 'exposes_gap': True}]

**Per-model derivation results:**
  - claude-opus-5: grounded=True, error=None, summary="Under Craddock, a Texas court should set aside a default judgment and grant a new trial when three elements are satisfied: (1) the defendant's failure to answer was not intentional or the result of conscious indifference but was due to mistake or accident, (2) the motion sets up a meritorious defense, and (3) it is filed at a time when granting it will cause no delay or other injury to the plaintiff. The motion is subject to strict timing: 30 days after the judgment is signed in district/county court (Rule 329b(a)), overruled by operation of law if not ruled on within 75 days (Rule 329b(c)), or 14 days with denial by operation of law at 5:00 p.m. on the 21st day in justice court (Rule 505.3); Rule 306a(4)-(5) can restart those periods (up to 90 days) if the party proves late notice or actual knowledge by sworn motion. Because the outcome turns on a fact-bound, multi-element weighing, it is genuinely discretionary — except that under Peralta a defendant never validly served need not show a meritorious defense, and such a defendant may instead use a restricted appeal within 6 months or a bill of review within 4 years."
  - gpt-5.5: grounded=True, error=None, summary='A Texas default judgment should be set aside and a new trial ordered when the defendant’s failure to answer was not intentional or due to conscious indifference, but resulted from mistake or accident, and the new-trial motion sets up a meritorious defense and can be granted without delay or injury to the plaintiff. The motion for new trial must be filed before or within 30 days after the judgment is signed, and if not ruled on by written order within 75 days after signing, it is overruled by operation of law.'
  - gemini-2.5-pro: grounded=True, error=None, summary="According to the provided text, a default judgment should be set aside and a new trial ordered if three conditions are met. The defendant's failure to answer must not have been intentional or the result of conscious indifference, but rather due to a mistake or accident. The defendant's motion must also set up a meritorious defense and be filed at a time when granting it would not cause delay or otherwise injure the plaintiff."

**File:** `rules/debt/state/california/ca_debt_state_layer_v1.json`

**Evidence:** 3 adversarial edge case(s) flagged as exposing a gap: [{'scenario': "A woman is served at her own home by a debt buyer's process server; service was textbook proper, but she says the account isn't hers (mistaken identity/identity theft — a different person with a similar name). She asks what to file.", 'realistic_and_common': True, 'would_cause_wrong_answer': True, 'exposes_gap': True, 'gap_description': "The node's defective-service branch is triggered by the phrase 'served on the wrong person,' which reads naturally onto the very common mistaken-identity debt-collection defendant. But a properly served non-debtor has no service/jurisdiction defect — her defense is on the merits and must be raised by ANSWER (general denial plus affirmative defenses). Steering her to a CCP § 418.10 motion to quash gets it denied and, because the motion tolls nothing after denial in the way she expects, risks default. The checklist item should distinguish 'disputes the manner/fact of service or jurisdictional contacts' from 'disputes liability on the debt.'"}, {'scenario': 'A man calls after his paycheck was garnished. He was allegedly substitute-served two years ago at an old address, never saw the papers, and a default judgment was entered. He wants to know his deadline to respond.', 'realistic_and_common': True, 'would_cause_wrong_answer': True, 'exposes_gap': True, 'gap_description': "The node treats the 30-day deadline as one-directional and describes missing it only as a consequence ('plaintiff may apply for default'), with no post-default path. Applied as encoded, it tells a very common caller — the sewer-service default-judgment debtor — that his window closed, when the actual answer is a motion to set aside under CCP § 473(b)/(d) or § 473.5 (two-year/180-day outer limits for defendants who never got actual notice), plus a claim of exemption for the garnishment. No checklist item asks whether default or judgment has already been entered, which is dispositive of what the person should do."}, {'scenario': 'An in-state California defendant receives the summons and complaint by first-class mail together with a Notice and Acknowledgment of Receipt form (CCP § 415.30) and never signs or returns it; he asks when his answer is due.', 'realistic_and_common': True, 'would_cause_wrong_answer': True, 'exposes_gap': True, 'gap_description': "The service-method list covers §§ 415.20, 415.40 (out-of-state mail) and 415.50 but omits § 415.30, the routine cost-saving in-state mail-with-acknowledgment method. Under § 415.30(c) service is complete only on the date the defendant signs and returns the acknowledgment; if he never signs, he is not served at all and no answer is due. The encoded logic would treat 'mail' as service date + 10 + 30 days, producing a deadline (and an obligation to answer, which is a general appearance) where none legally exists."}]

**Per-model derivation results:**
  - claude-opus-5: grounded=True, error=None, summary="Under Cal. Code Civ. Proc. § 412.20(a)(3)-(4) and (a)(6), the summons must direct the defendant to file a written pleading responding to the complaint within 30 days after the summons is served, and must warn that otherwise a default may be entered and the court may decide against the defendant without hearing them, potentially leading to wage garnishment or seizure of money or property. When the 30 days begins depends on when service is 'complete': for substituted service and out-of-state service by mail, service is deemed complete on the 10th day after mailing (§§ 415.20, 415.40), and for service by publication it is deemed complete on the 28th day after the first publication (§ 415.50). Instead of (or in addition to) a pleading, a defendant may, on or before the last day to plead — or within further time the court allows for good cause — file a motion to quash service for lack of jurisdiction under § 418.10, which does not count as an appearance unless the motion is denied."
  - gpt-5.5: grounded=True, error=None, summary='A California summons must direct the defendant to file a written pleading in response to the complaint within 30 days after the summons is served. If the defendant does not respond within that time, default may be entered and the plaintiff may seek the relief demanded in the complaint, including wage garnishment or taking money or property. For substituted service and service outside California by mail, service is deemed complete on the 10th day after mailing; for publication, service is deemed complete on the 28th day after the first day of publication.'
  - gemini-2.5-pro: grounded=True, error=None, summary='A defendant must file a written pleading in response to a complaint within 30 days after the summons is served. The date service is considered "complete," and thus when the 30-day clock begins, depends on the method of service. For substituted service or service by mail outside of California, service is deemed complete 10 days after mailing; for service by publication, it is deemed complete on the 28th day after the first publication.'

**Evidence:** 3 adversarial edge case(s) flagged as exposing a gap: [{'scenario': "A debtor's bank account is frozen not by a private judgment creditor but by the California Franchise Tax Board for unpaid state income tax (or by the IRS under a federal notice of levy). The debtor asks whether the automatic minimum protects the balance.", 'realistic_and_common': True, 'would_cause_wrong_answer': True, 'exposes_gap': "Bank levies by taxing authorities are among the most common account freezes consumers experience. The node's only carve-outs are judgments for wages owed, child support, and spousal support; it frames the levy as an 'ordinary judgment-creditor levy' but nothing in the logic or checklist asks who is levying or under what authority. FTB collection under Rev. & Tax. Code §§19262/19271 and IRS levies under 26 U.S.C. §6331 are not governed by the CCP §704 exemption scheme (the IRS has its own limited exemptions, none of which is a bank-balance minimum), so the node would tell a tax-levy debtor their balance is automatically protected up to the W&IC figure when it is not.", 'gap_description': 'No checklist item asks whether the levy is a civil judgment-creditor levy versus an administrative tax levy (FTB/IRS/EDD) or other non-CCP enforcement (e.g., federal criminal restitution), for which the CCP §704 exemptions do not apply.'}, {'scenario': "A debtor's account is levied; the balance consists mainly of directly-deposited California unemployment insurance and State Disability Insurance benefits, plus a small pension/401(k) distribution deposited the prior month. The debtor has no Social Security and no CalWORKs-type aid.", 'realistic_and_common': True, 'would_cause_wrong_answer': True, 'exposes_gap': "This is a very common levy profile, and the node's protections would materially understate what the debtor can protect. CCP §704.080's 'public benefits' definition is narrow (aid under the W&IC public assistance chapters); UI/SDI benefits are exempt under CCP §704.120 and pension/retirement funds under §704.115, both of which trace into deposit accounts. The node and checklist enumerate only Social Security/public benefits, recent wages, and need-based §704.225, so a UI/SDI or pension recipient would be told the only automatic protection is the small W&IC minimum when a much larger (often complete) exemption exists.", 'gap_description': "The node's list of source-traced exemptions is incomplete: no coverage of CCP §704.120 (unemployment/disability/union benefits), §704.115 (retirement plans and traceable distributions), §704.140 (personal injury awards), or §704.160 (workers' compensation), all of which routinely sit in a levied deposit account."}, {'scenario': "A wage earner's account is levied on Monday; the balance is a paycheck deposited the prior Friday. The debtor reads the node, learns a §704.070 claim gives 75-100% protection, and files the claim of exemption with the levying officer three weeks after being served with the notice of levy.", 'realistic_and_common': True, 'would_cause_wrong_answer': True, 'exposes_gap': "The node expressly identifies the §704.070 recent-wage claim as 'the most common real-world bank-levy scenario' and as requiring a 'timely-filed claim,' but neither the logic nor the checklist encodes the deadline or mechanics: CCP §703.520 requires the claim (with a financial statement for need-based claims like §704.225) to be filed with the levying officer within 15 days of personal service (20 if mailed), and the levying officer releases funds to the creditor after roughly 20 days absent a claim. A debtor who acts on the node's substantive advice but misses the short window loses the entire exemption, so the encoded answer ('you can claim 75-100%') is affirmatively misleading without the timing.", 'gap_description': "No checklist item or logic entry captures the CCP §703.520/§703.550 claim-filing deadline, the financial-statement requirement, or the creditor's opposition/hearing procedure — the procedural steps on which the node's own largest identified protections actually depend."}]

**Per-model derivation results:**
  - claude-opus-5: grounded=True, error=None, summary="Yes — California law gives an automatic, no-claim-required exemption for a baseline amount of money in a judgment debtor's deposit account. Under Cal. Code Civ. Proc. § 704.220(a), money in the debtor's deposit account up to the minimum basic standard of adequate care for a family of four for Region 1 (set by Welf. & Inst. Code § 11452 and annually adjusted by the State Department of Social Services under § 11453) is exempt without making a claim; the exemption does not apply, however, to levies to satisfy judgments for wages owed (including damages and penalties), child support, or spousal support (§ 704.220(c)(1)). Separate automatic dollar-amount exemptions apply to accounts receiving direct deposits of public benefits ($1,750 one depositor / $2,600 two or more) or social security benefits ($3,500 / $5,250) under § 704.080(b), and amounts not otherwise exempt may still be exempt to the extent necessary for the support of the debtor and the debtor's spouse and dependents under § 704.225. All these exemptions apply only to property of a natural person (§ 703.020(a))."
  - gpt-5.5: grounded=True, error=None, summary='California exempts, without the debtor making a claim, money in a judgment debtor’s deposit account up to the amount equal to the minimum basic standard of adequate care for a family of four for Region 1, as established and annually adjusted under the cited Welfare and Institutions Code provisions. This automatic exemption does not apply to levies for judgments for wages owed, child support, or spousal support, and the chapter’s exemptions apply only to property of a natural person.'
  - gemini-2.5-pro: grounded=True, error=None, summary="An amount of money in a judgment debtor's deposit account is automatically exempt without the debtor needing to make a claim. The exempt amount is equal to or less than the minimum basic standard of adequate care for a family of four in Region 1, as annually adjusted by the State Department of Social Services. However, this automatic exemption does not apply to a levy intended to satisfy a judgment for owed wages, child support, or spousal support."

Applied as encoded, the rule would tell this debtor she is protected up to the county-median figure (potentially $600k+) when federal law caps her at roughly a third of that — a materially wrong answer.", 'exposes_gap': True}, {'scenario': 'A homeowner is facing a forced judicial sale of his residence, but the claim being enforced is a judgment for child and spousal support arrears held by his ex-spouse; a second homeowner in the same posture faces a recorded federal tax lien and threatened IRS foreclosure on the residence.', 'realistic_and_common': True, 'would_cause_wrong_answer': True, 'gap_description': "The checklist omits any dispositive fact about the nature of the claim or lien. So the floor is $300,000 and the ceiling is $600,000, with the actual figure depending on the county's prior-year median home price. Subdivision (b) provides that these amounts adjust annually for inflation starting January 1, 2022, using the change in the annual California Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers (published by the Department of Industrial Relations) for the most recent one-year period ending December 31 before the adjustment, rounded to the nearest $25.   - gemini-2.5-pro: grounded=True, error=None, summary="The California homestead exemption is the greater of two amounts: either $300,000, or the county's median sale price for a single-family home from the previous year, with the latter amount being capped at $600,000. Applying the weekly formula to a biweekly or monthly disposable-earnings figure inflates the exempt floor calculation asymmetrically (48 x minimum wage is subtracted only once instead of twice/4.33 times) and produces a materially wrong maximum-garnishment dollar figure. Pay period is dispositive, not merely informational."}, {'scenario': "A worker grosses $1,000/week; after federal and state taxes, SDI, a voluntary 401(k) contribution, health insurance premiums, and union dues, his take-home is $620. Lay users almost universally equate disposable earnings with take-home pay, and the node's silence lets that error pass through to an understated cap, so a debtor would wrongly conclude a lawful withholding is unlawful (or a creditor would under-withhold)."}, {'scenario': 'A debtor already has an active child support earnings withholding order taking 25% of his disposable earnings when a consumer-debt creditor serves a wage garnishment. Concurrent support orders are extremely common among consumer judgment debtors, so the encoded answer materially overstates what the consumer creditor may lawfully collect."}]

**Per-model derivation results:**
  - claude-opus-5: grounded=True, error=None, summary="Under § 706.050(a), the maximum amount of a judgment debtor's disposable earnings for any workweek that may be taken under an earnings withholding order is the lesser of (1) 20% of that week's disposable earnings, or (2) 40% of the amount by which that week's disposable earnings exceed 48 times the applicable minimum hourly wage. If the debtor works where a local minimum wage exceeds the state minimum wage, the higher local minimum wage is used in the second calculation. This formula applies except as otherwise provided in the chapter, and it became operative September 1, 2023 (§ 706.050(c)); the excerpt states a general cap and does not set out any separate figure specific to consumer-debt judgments."
  - gpt-5.5: grounded=True, error=None, summary='Operative September 1, 2023, the maximum amount of an individual judgment debtor’s disposable earnings for a workweek that may be levied under an earnings withholding order is the lesser of: 20% of disposable earnings for that week, or 40% of the amount by which disposable earnings exceed 48 times the applicable minimum hourly wage. If the debtor works where the local minimum wage is higher than the state minimum wage, the local minimum wage is used for that calculation.'
  - gemini-2.5-pro: grounded=True, error=None, summary="Effective September 1, 2023, the maximum amount of an individual's disposable earnings that can be garnished in a workweek is the lesser of two amounts. The first option is 20% of the individual's disposable earnings for that week. Proc. § 337(a), an action on a contract, obligation, or liability founded on a written instrument must be brought within four years (with a special three-month limit for deficiency-type money judgments after a trustee's sale under a deed of trust or mortgage with power of sale). Once that four-year period runs, § 337(d) forbids filing suit, arbitration, or any other legal proceeding to collect the debt, and the period may be extended only under § 360 — i.e., by a written acknowledgment or promise signed by the debtor, or by a payment of principal or interest, though no payment can revive a claim already barred. If the cause of action arose in another state or country and is time-barred there, § 361 bars suit in California as well, except in favor of a person who has been a California citizen holding the claim since it accrued. Separately, a money judgment is enforceable for 10 years from entry and may be renewed within that period under § 683.020."
  - gpt-5.5: grounded=True, error=None, summary='An action on a contract, obligation, or liability founded on a written instrument must be brought within four years. Once that period has run, a person may not bring suit or initiate arbitration or another legal proceeding to collect the debt, and any extension is only as provided in Section 360. Section 360 requires a signed written acknowledgment or promise, and a payment on a promissory note may stop the running of time but does not revive a cause of action that is already barred.'
  - gemini-2.5-pro: grounded=True, error=None, summary='Under California law, a lawsuit based on a written contract must be brought within four years. After this period has run, a person is barred from bringing a suit or other legal proceeding to collect the debt. **Per-model derivation results:**
  - claude-opus-5: grounded=True, error=None, summary="15 U.S.C. § 1692f bars a debt collector from using unfair or unconscionable means to collect any debt, and its non-exhaustive list ('without limiting the general application of the foregoing') identifies eight specific violations: (1) collecting amounts (including interest, fees, charges, or incidental expenses) not expressly authorized by the debt agreement or permitted by law; (2) accepting a check postdated more than five days without written notice of intent to deposit sent 3–10 business days before deposit; (3) soliciting postdated instruments to threaten or institute criminal prosecution; (4) depositing or threatening to deposit a postdated instrument early; (5) causing a person to incur communication charges by concealing the communication's true purpose; (6) taking or threatening nonjudicial dispossession or disablement of property where there is no present right to possession through an enforceable security interest, no present intent to take possession, or the property is legally exempt; (7) communicating with a consumer about a debt by post card; and (8) using language or symbols other than the collector's address on envelopes (a business name is allowed only if it does not indicate the debt collection business). The prohibitions apply only to a 'debt collector' as defined in § 1692a(6), which excludes, among others, persons collecting debts they originated, debts not in default when obtained, and debts obtained as a secured party in a commercial credit transaction."
  - gpt-5.5: grounded=True, error=None, summary='A debt collector may not use unfair or unconscionable means to collect or attempt to collect a debt. The statute lists violations including collecting unauthorized amounts; mishandling or soliciting postdated payment instruments in specified ways; causing communication charges by concealing the communication’s true purpose; taking or threatening certain nonjudicial dispossession actions without a present right, intent, or where property is exempt; communicating by postcard; and using improper language or symbols on mailed envelopes. The term “debt collector” is defined to cover businesses principally engaged in collecting debts or regularly collecting debts owed to another, subject to stated exclusions.'
  - gemini-2.5-pro: grounded=True, error=None, summary='A debt collector may not use unfair or unconscionable means to collect a debt. Prohibited conduct includes collecting any amount not expressly authorized by the agreement or permitted by law, and improperly handling postdated checks, such as depositing them without proper notice or soliciting them to threaten criminal prosecution. It is also a violation to threaten repossession of property without a present right to do so, communicate about a debt via postcard, or use revealing language or symbols on an envelope.'

  - claude-opus-5: grounded=True, error=None, summary="Under 12 C.F.R. § 1006.14(b)(2)(i), a debt collector is presumed to comply with the harassment prohibition in § 1006.14(b)(1) and FDCPA § 806(5) if, as to a particular person and a particular debt, it places no more than seven telephone calls within seven consecutive days, and places no call within the seven consecutive days following a telephone conversation with that person about the debt (the conversation date counts as day one). Exceeding either of those frequencies flips the presumption: under § 1006.14(b)(2)(ii) the collector is presumed to violate § 1006.14(b)(1) and FDCPA § 806(5). Both presumptions are subject to the call-type exclusions in paragraph (b)(3). Because the limits are measured per 'particular debt,' § 1006.14(b)(4) counts each of a consumer's debts separately, except that student loan debts serviced under a single account number when obtained by the collector count as one debt."

A debt collector is presumed to violate the rule if it exceeds either of those call-frequency limits; “particular debt” means each debt in collection, with a special rule treating certain student loans serviced under one account number as one particular debt.'

For the purposes of this rule, each of a consumer's debts is considered a 'particular debt,' except for student loans, where all loans under a single account number are treated as one debt."

All of these steps must be completed within the same period § 1681i(a)(1) gives the bureau to finish its reinvestigation (§ 1681s-2(b)(2)). Courts have read a reasonableness requirement into the investigation duty, rejecting mere 'data conformity' checks of the furnisher's own database that ignore the consumer's supporting documents, with some circuits applying an 'objectively and readily verifiable' framing. Unlike subsection (a) duties (which are enforced exclusively by federal and state officials per § 1681s-2(c)–(d)), subsection (b) duties are privately enforceable: willful violations expose the furnisher to actual or statutory damages of $100–$1,000 plus punitive damages, fees and costs (§ 1681n), negligent violations to actual damages plus fees and costs (§ 1681o), with suit brought within 2 years of discovery or 5 years of the violation (§ 1681p)."

These investigations, reviews, and reports must be completed within the time period applicable to the consumer reporting agency’s reinvestigation, and courts read the investigation duty as requiring reasonableness rather than a mere internal “data conformity” check.'

Code § 42.001(a), the categories of personal property listed in § 42.002(a) — such as home furnishings, provisions, farm and ranch implements, trade tools (including boats and motor vehicles used in a trade), clothing, jewelry (capped at 25% of the aggregate limit), two firearms, sporting equipment, one motor vehicle per licensed family member or single adult, specified livestock and fowl, and household pets — are exempt from garnishment, attachment, execution, or other seizure so long as their aggregate fair market value (excluding liens and encumbrances) does not exceed $100,000 for a family or $50,000 for a single adult. Section 42.001(b) exempts additional property outside that cap: current wages for personal services (except for court-ordered child support), professionally prescribed health aids, alimony/support/separate maintenance, and a religious bible or sacred text (except when seized by a landlord exercising lease-related seizure rights). Section 42.0021 separately exempts, in addition to the cap, assets in and payments from tax-qualified or tax-deferred retirement and savings plans, with distributions exempt for 60 days and indefinitely if rolled over in a nontaxable rollover. These exemptions do not apply to child support liens under Family Code Chapter 157, Subchapter G (§ 42.005), and property acquired or improved with nonexempt assets to defraud, delay, or hinder a creditor loses its exempt status, though a creditor must assert that claim within two years of acquisition (§ 42.004).'
  - gpt-5.5: grounded=True, error=None, summary='Texas exempts certain personal property described in § 42.002 from garnishment, attachment, execution, or other seizure up to an aggregate fair market value of $100,000 for a family or $50,000 for a single adult who is not a member of a family, excluding liens and other encumbrances. Certain items, including current wages, prescribed health aids, support payments, and certain sacred religious writings, are exempt and do not count toward those aggregate caps. Retirement and similar tax-exempt or tax-deferred plan assets are also exempt in addition to the general cap, but these exemptions do not apply to child support liens and may be lost for property acquired with intent to defraud, delay, or hinder an interested person.'
  - gemini-2.5-pro: grounded=True, error=None, summary='Texas law protects personal property from creditor seizure up to an aggregate fair market value of $100,000 for a family or $50,000 for a single adult, exclusive of liens. This limit applies to a specific list of property types, including home furnishings, vehicles, and tools of a trade. Certain other assets, such as current wages, professionally prescribed health aids, and most retirement accounts, are fully exempt and do not count toward these dollar limits. However, these exemptions do not apply to child support liens or to property acquired with the intent to defraud creditors.'

Code § 41.001(a), a homestead (and burial lots) is exempt from seizure for creditors' claims, except for encumbrances properly fixed on the homestead property; § 41.002 caps the exemption at up to 10 acres for an urban home, and for a rural home up to 200 acres for a family or 100 acres for a single adult. In bankruptcy, the exemption is limited by federal law: 11 U.S.C. § 522(p) bars exempting homestead interest acquired within 1,215 days before filing above the statutory cap (base $250,000; currently $214,000 for cases filed April 1, 2025–March 31, 2028), and § 522(o) reduces the exempt value attributable to non-exempt property the debtor disposed of within 10 years before filing with intent to hinder, delay, or defraud creditors. The exemption also does not defeat federal tax liens: under 26 U.S.C. § 6321 and United States v. Rodgers, a federal tax lien attaches to all property of a delinquent taxpayer notwithstanding the state homestead exemption (Supremacy Clause), and a federal court may order judicial sale of the homestead to satisfy the lien."
  - gpt-5.5: grounded=True, error=None, summary='Texas law exempts a homestead, and one or more burial lots, from seizure for creditor claims except for properly fixed encumbrances on the homestead. The exemption is subject to acreage limits: an urban homestead may be up to 10 acres, while a rural homestead may be up to 200 acres for a family or 100 acres for a single adult. In bankruptcy, federal limits and reductions may apply to recently acquired homestead interests or fraudulent conversions, and federal tax liens can attach to and be enforced against homestead property despite the state exemption.'
  - gemini-2.5-pro: grounded=True, error=None, summary="Under Texas law, a homestead is generally exempt from seizure by creditors, with size limits of 10 acres for an urban home and up to 200 acres for a rural family home. This exemption does not apply to properly fixed encumbrances on the property. Furthermore, the state exemption is overcome by a federal tax lien, which can result in a court-ordered sale of the property. In a federal bankruptcy context, the exemption's value can also be limited if the debtor recently acquired a large interest or fraudulently transferred other assets into the homestead."

Timing is strict: the motion must be filed within 30 days after the judgment is signed (14 days in justice court under Rule 505.3), and it is overruled by operation of law if not ruled on by written order within 75 days (21 days under Rule 505.3(d)); Rule 306a(4)-(5) can restart those periods, on sworn motion and proof, from the date of notice or actual knowledge, but never more than 90 days after signing. If service was defective or absent, Peralta means the defendant need not show a meritorious defense, and if the new-trial window has passed the defendant may instead seek a restricted appeal within 6 months or a bill of review within 4 years.'
  - gpt-5.5: grounded=True, error=None, summary='Under Craddock, a Texas default judgment should be set aside and a new trial ordered when the defendant’s failure to answer was not intentional or due to conscious indifference, but resulted from mistake or accident, so long as the new-trial motion sets up a meritorious defense and granting it will not delay or injure the plaintiff. The motion must also be filed within the applicable deadline, generally within 30 days after the judgment is signed, subject to the stated notice-restart rule in Rule 306a; different shorter deadlines apply under Rule 505.3. If the defendant was never properly served, the text states that due process does not require showing the Craddock meritorious-defense element, and other post-deadline remedies may be available.'
  - gemini-2.5-pro: grounded=True, error=None, summary="A default judgment should be set aside and a new trial ordered if the defendant meets a three-part test. First, the defendant's failure to answer must not have been intentional or from conscious indifference, but rather due to a mistake or accident. Second, the motion for a new trial must set up a meritorious defense. Third, the motion must be filed at a time when granting it will not cause delay or otherwise injure the plaintiff."

When the 30 days begins depends on how service was completed: substituted service and out-of-state service by mail are deemed complete on the 10th day after mailing (§§ 415.20, 415.40), and service by publication is deemed complete on the 28th day after the first publication (§ 415.50). Instead of (or in addition to) answering, a defendant may, on or before the last day to plead (or within further time allowed by the court for good cause), serve and file a motion to quash service for lack of jurisdiction without that constituting an appearance unless the motion is denied (§ 418.10).'

Proc. § 337(a), an action on a contract, obligation, or liability founded on a written instrument must be brought within four years (with a special three-month limit for deficiency-type money judgments after a nonjudicial trustee's sale under a deed of trust or mortgage with power of sale). Section 337(d) provides that once that period has run, no one may sue, arbitrate, or start any other legal proceeding to collect the debt, and the period may be extended only under § 360. Section 360 allows extension only by a written acknowledgment or promise signed by the party charged, or by a payment of principal or interest on a promissory note (which stops the running from the time of each payment), but expressly states no payment by itself revives an already time-barred claim. Additionally, § 361 bars suit in California on a cause of action that arose in another state or country and is already time-barred there, unless the plaintiff has been a California citizen holding the claim since it accrued."
  - gpt-5.5: grounded=True, error=None, summary='An action on a contract, obligation, or liability founded on a written instrument must be brought within four years. Section 360 requires a signed written acknowledgment or promise, or certain payments on a promissory note before the claim is barred; a payment by itself does not revive a claim once barred.'
  - gemini-2.5-pro: grounded=True, error=None, summary='An action upon a contract or liability founded upon a written instrument must be brought within four years. Once this period has run, a person is prohibited from bringing a suit or other legal proceeding to collect the debt. The time limit can be extended by a signed written acknowledgment or promise, or in some cases by making a payment, but a payment cannot revive a debt that is already barred.'

  - claude-opus-5: grounded=True, error=None, summary="Under 15 U.S.C. § 1692g(a), a debt collector must, in the initial communication or in a written notice sent within five days after it, tell the consumer: the amount of the debt; the name of the creditor owed; that the debt will be assumed valid unless disputed within thirty days after receipt of the notice; that a written dispute within that thirty-day period will cause the collector to obtain and mail verification of the debt or a copy of a judgment; and that on written request within that period the collector will give the name and address of the original creditor. Regulation F (12 C.F.R. § 1006.34(a)(1), (c)) allows the information to be given orally in the initial communication or by a validation notice in or within five days of it, and expands the required content to include the debt-collector communication disclosure, detailed debt information (collector and consumer identification, current creditor, itemization date and amounts, current amount), consumer-protection information (including the dispute-window end date, assumption of validity, original-creditor-request right, and CFPB website), and consumer-response prompts and addresses. The 30-day clock runs from the consumer's receipt of the notice: § 1006.34(b)(5) defines the validation period as starting when the collector provides the information and ending 30 days after the consumer receives or is assumed to receive it, and the collector may assume receipt on any date at least five days (excluding Saturdays, Sundays, and legal public holidays) after it provides the information. Under § 1692g(b), a written dispute or original-creditor request within that period requires the collector to cease collection of the disputed debt until verification, a judgment copy, or the original creditor's name and address is mailed to the consumer; otherwise, non-violative collection activity may continue during the 30-day period."
  - gpt-5.5: grounded=True, error=None, summary='A debt collector must provide validation information in the initial communication, within five days after the initial communication, or orally in the initial communication if permitted. The notice must include the debt amount, creditor name, statements about the consumer’s right to dispute within 30 days after receipt, verification/judgment mailing if disputed in writing, and original-creditor information upon timely written request; the regulation also lists validation information categories including debt details, consumer protections, and response prompts. The validation period starts when the debt collector provides the validation information and ends 30 days after the consumer receives or is assumed to receive it; for calculating the assumed receipt date, the collector may assume receipt on any date at least five days after providing it, excluding legal public holidays, Saturdays, and Sundays.'
  - gemini-2.5-pro: grounded=True, error=None, summary="A debt validation notice must contain specific information, including the debt amount, the current creditor's name, and statements explaining the consumer's rights to dispute the debt and request verification. The 30-day period for a consumer to dispute the debt begins after they receive this notice. For the purpose of calculating this deadline, a debt collector may assume the consumer received the notice five days (excluding weekends and public holidays) after the collector sent it."

These include collecting amounts (interest, fees, charges, expenses) not expressly authorized by the debt agreement or permitted by law; accepting checks postdated more than five days without written notice of intent to deposit sent 3–10 business days beforehand; soliciting postdated instruments to threaten or institute criminal prosecution; depositing or threatening to deposit a postdated instrument early; causing a person to be charged for communications by concealing the communication's true purpose; and taking or threatening nonjudicial dispossession or disablement of property when there is no present right to possession through an enforceable security interest, no present intention to take possession, or the property is legally exempt. It also bars communicating with a consumer about a debt by postcard and using any language or symbol on a mailed envelope or telegram other than the collector's address, except a business name that does not reveal it is in the debt collection business."

  - gemini-2.5-pro: grounded=True, error=None, summary='Unfair or unconscionable debt collection methods are prohibited. The statute catalogs violations including false claims of government affiliation, misstating the character/amount/legal status of a debt, falsely implying attorney involvement, threatening unlawful or unintended action, misrepresenting legal process, using a false business name, communicating false credit information, and failing to give required debt-collection disclosures. The provided regulatory text specifies the required initial and subsequent communication disclosures, while the supplied judicial gloss states that actionable misstatements must be material and that threatening or pursuing known time-barred debt can violate the statute.'

For a false statement to be actionable, courts require it to be "material," meaning it must be capable of influencing a consumer\'s decision regarding the debt. **Per-model derivation results:**
  - claude-opus-5: grounded=True, error=None, summary="Once a furnisher receives notice of a dispute from a consumer reporting agency under § 1681i(a)(2), § 1681s-2(b)(1) requires it to investigate the disputed information, review all relevant information the CRA forwarded, report the investigation's results back to the CRA, and — if the information is found incomplete or inaccurate — report that to all other nationwide CRAs it furnished the information to; if an item is inaccurate, incomplete, or unverifiable, the furnisher must promptly modify, delete, or permanently block reporting of it. Under § 1681s-2(b)(2), all of these investigations, reviews, and reports must be completed within the same time period § 1681i(a)(1) gives the CRA to finish its reinvestigation. Although the statute does not say 'reasonable,' courts have read a reasonableness requirement into the investigation duty and have rejected mere 'data conformity' checks (comparing internal records to what was already reported, without reviewing the consumer's supporting documents) as sufficient, with some circuits applying an 'objectively and readily verifiable' framing. Notably, these subsection (b) duties are the privately enforceable ones — subsections (c) and (d) strip private liability under §§ 1681n and 1681o for violations of subsection (a), which is enforced exclusively by federal and state officials."
  - gpt-5.5: grounded=True, error=None, summary='After receiving notice from a consumer reporting agency of a dispute about the completeness or accuracy of information it furnished, the furnisher must investigate the disputed information, review all relevant information provided by the agency, and report the investigation results back to the agency. If the information is incomplete, inaccurate, or cannot be verified, the furnisher must promptly modify, delete, or permanently block reporting of the item, and must report incomplete or inaccurate results to other nationwide consumer reporting agencies to which it furnished the information. The required investigation, review, and reports must be completed within the same period applicable to the consumer reporting agency under § 1681i(a)(1), and courts read the investigation duty as requiring reasonableness rather than mere data-conformity checking.'
  - gemini-2.5-pro: grounded=True, error=None, summary="After a consumer disputes information through a credit bureau, the furnisher must conduct a reasonable investigation, which means more than just checking its own records against what was reported. The furnisher must also review all information from the credit bureau, report its findings back, and complete these actions within the bureau's own deadline. If the information is found to be inaccurate, incomplete, or unverifiable, the furnisher must report the results to all nationwide bureaus and promptly modify, delete, or block the item."

**Per-model derivation results:**
  - claude-opus-5: grounded=True, error=None, summary="Once a furnisher receives notice from a consumer reporting agency that a consumer disputes the completeness or accuracy of information the furnisher provided, § 1681s-2(b)(1) requires it to investigate the disputed item, review all relevant information the agency forwarded with the notice, and report the investigation's results back to that agency. If the investigation shows the information is incomplete or inaccurate, the furnisher must also report that to all other nationwide consumer reporting agencies it supplied, and if the item is found inaccurate, incomplete, or unverifiable, it must promptly modify, delete, or permanently block reporting of the item. If the disputed item is inaccurate, incomplete, or cannot be verified after reinvestigation, the furnisher must promptly modify, delete, or permanently block reporting of that item, and must complete the required investigations, reviews, and reports within the time period applicable to the consumer reporting agency’s required actions.'

Code § 42.001(a), the personal property listed in § 42.002 is exempt from garnishment, attachment, execution, or other seizure up to an aggregate fair market value of $100,000 for property provided for a family, or $50,000 for a single adult who is not a family member, in each case excluding liens, security interests, or other encumbrances. Section 42.002(a) identifies the covered categories, including home furnishings and heirlooms, food provisions, farm/ranch vehicles and implements, trade tools and equipment (including boats and motor vehicles used in a trade or profession), clothing, jewelry (capped at 25% of the aggregate limit), two firearms, athletic and sporting equipment, one motor vehicle per licensed family member or single adult (or a non-licensed person who relies on another to drive), specified numbers of livestock and fowl with forage, and household pets. Section 42.001(b) exempts certain property entirely outside the dollar caps: current wages for personal services (except for court-ordered child support enforcement), professionally prescribed health aids, alimony/support/separate maintenance, and a religious bible or sacred-writings book (except when seized by a landlord exercising rights after a lease breach or abandonment).'
  - gpt-5.5: grounded=True, error=None, summary='Texas exempts certain personal property from garnishment, attachment, execution, or other seizure if it is property described in Section 42.002 and its aggregate fair market value does not exceed $100,000 for a family or $50,000 for a single adult who is not a family member, excluding liens and other charges. Some property—such as current wages, prescribed health aids, support payments, and certain sacred writings—is exempt from seizure and does not count toward those aggregate limits. Section 42.002 lists exempt categories including home furnishings, provisions, work tools and vehicles, apparel, limited jewelry, firearms, sporting equipment, certain motor vehicles, specified livestock, and household pets.'
  - gemini-2.5-pro: grounded=True, error=None, summary='In Texas, certain personal property is exempt from seizure by creditors up to an aggregate fair market value of $100,000 for a family or $50,000 for a single adult, exclusive of any liens. The types of property subject to this limit include home furnishings, tools of a trade, motor vehicles, and certain animals. Additionally, some property like current wages, professionally prescribed health aids, and alimony is completely exempt from seizure and does not count toward these aggregate value limits.'

If any of those elements is missing or the notice was late, the notice is not compliant. On timing, the dispute window (the 'validation period') runs from the date the validation information is provided until 30 days after the consumer receives or is assumed to receive it, and the collector may assume receipt on any date at least five business days (excluding Saturdays, Sundays, and legal public holidays) after it provided the notice (12 C.F.R. § 1006.34(b)(5)). So the consumer is still within the window if today's date falls on or before that 30-day end date; a written dispute or original-creditor request sent within it requires the collector to cease collection until verification or the creditor's name and address is mailed (15 U.S.C. § 1692g(b)). The provided text supplies the standard, but the actual dates of the initial communication and notice are needed to fix the exact window."

If any of these items is missing or was not delivered in the required time frame, the notice is not compliant. The dispute window runs from the date the validation information is provided until 30 days after the consumer receives (or is assumed to receive) it, and the collector may assume receipt on any date at least five business days — excluding Saturdays, Sundays, and legal public holidays — after providing it (12 C.F.R. § 1006.34(b)(5)); so the consumer is still within the window if that 30-day period has not yet run. If the consumer disputes in writing within that period, the collector must cease collection of the disputed portion until it mails verification or the original creditor's name and address (15 U.S.C. § 1692g(b)). The text supplies the standard but no facts about the particular notice or dates, so the actual compliance and timeliness conclusions depend on applying these rules to the specific mailing and dispute dates."

The statute then lists non-exhaustive examples of violations, including collecting any amount (interest, fees, charges, expenses) not expressly authorized by the debt agreement or permitted by law; accepting a check postdated more than five days without written notice of intent to deposit sent 3–10 business days beforehand; soliciting postdated instruments to threaten or institute criminal prosecution; depositing or threatening to deposit a postdated instrument early; causing a person to be charged for communications by concealing the communication's true purpose; and taking or threatening nonjudicial repossession or disablement of property where there is no present right to possession through an enforceable security interest, no present intention to take possession, or the property is exempt by law. It also bars communicating with a consumer about a debt by post card and using any language or symbol other than the collector's address on mail or telegram envelopes, except a business name that does not indicate the debt collection business."
  - gpt-5.5: grounded=True, error=None, summary='A debt collector may not use unfair or unconscionable means to collect or attempt to collect a debt.   - gemini-2.5-pro: grounded=True, error=None, summary='The statute forbids debt collectors from using unfair or unconscionable methods to collect a debt. Under (b)(2)(ii), exceeding either of those frequencies creates a presumption that the collector violated the rule and FDCPA § 806(5). Both presumptions are subject to the call-type exclusions in paragraph (b)(3). Per (b)(4), the limits are applied per 'particular debt' — each debt in collection separately — except that student loan debts serviced under a single account number when obtained by the collector are treated as one particular debt."

A “particular debt” means each debt in collection, except that certain student loan debts serviced under a single account number are treated together as one particular debt.'
  - gemini-2.5-pro: grounded=True, error=None, summary='A debt collector is presumed to have engaged in unlawful conduct if, in connection with a particular debt, they call a person more than seven times within seven consecutive days. Procedurally, the motion must be filed before or within thirty days after the judgment is signed (Rule 329b(a)), and if the court does not sign a written order deciding it within seventy-five days of the judgment, it is overruled by operation of law (Rule 329b(c)). ## Archived -- Pre-Fix Diagnostic Runs (archived 2026-08-28)

**Why archived, not resolved:** every entry below comes from live corroboration runs (`run_20260826T171949Z` through
`run_20260826T202441Z`, all 2026-08-26 evening) that predate this project's Round 12-15 citation and tooling fixes:
the User-Agent fix and eCFR/FindLaw/Justia normalization work (Round 11), the CA re-pin to leginfo.legislature.ca.gov
and eCFR ellipsis fix (Round 12), the TX re-pin to statutes.capitol.texas.gov + Craddock re-pin to CourtListener plus
retry-on-flake logic (Round 13), and the FDCPA (b)(4) diagnostic pinpoint (Round 14). Nearly every CITATION-CHECK-FAILED
entry below traces to a since-fixed cause: a 403 from a bot-blocking secondary aggregator (FindLaw/Justia -- now re-pinned
to primary/Tier-A sources for CA and TX; AZ/UT/NY re-pins are still open work), a stale User-Agent, or an eCFR
normalization edge case. Nearly every MODEL-DISAGREEMENT entry below rests solely on the numeric/citation-fingerprint
heuristic, which Round 11 demoted to a secondary, non-gating diagnostic in favor of LLM-judged semantic agreement --
so a fingerprint mismatch alone, on its own, is no longer this project's disagreement signal.

None of this is an attorney-level resolution and none of these entries are being asserted correct -- they are being
moved out of the Open lane because the pipeline conditions that produced them no longer reflect the current rule
files or runner. Per the append-only discipline, nothing is deleted: every entry, its per-model derivations, and its
blank Resolution/Resolved-by/Date fields are preserved verbatim below. If the upcoming full-corpus live run reproduces
any of the same MODEL-DISAGREEMENT findings under the current LLM-judge, or a genuine (non-pipeline) CITATION-CHECK-FAILED
persists after the AZ/UT/NY re-pins land, it will re-file as a fresh entry in the Open section above, on its own
merits, under current tooling.

**Archived by:** Claude (Cowork) -- **Date:** 2026-08-28 -- per Andy's 2026-08-26 evening directive item 3
("confirmation that the disagreement queue is clean of runs 1-2 artifacts so the new flags land in an empty lane").

Conversely, under (b)(2)(ii), exceeding either of those frequencies creates a presumption of violation. The counts are applied per 'particular debt,' meaning each individual debt in collection, except that for student loans all debts serviced under a single account number when obtained by the collector count as one debt (§ 1006.14(b)(4))."
  - gpt-5.5: grounded=True, error=None, summary='A debt collector is presumed to comply if, for a particular person and a particular debt, it does not place more than seven calls within seven consecutive days and does not call within seven consecutive days after having had a telephone conversation with that person about that debt. A debt collector is presumed to violate the rule if it exceeds either of those call-frequency limits. For this purpose, a “particular debt” generally means each debt in collection, with a special rule grouping certain student loan debts serviced under a single account number.'
  - gemini-2.5-pro: grounded=True, error=None, summary="A debt collector is presumed to have violated the law if, in connection with a particular debt, they call a person more than seven times within seven consecutive days. Certain items, including current wages for personal services, prescribed health aids, support payments, and religious sacred writings, are exempt and are not counted toward those aggregate limits.'
  - gemini-2.5-pro: grounded=True, error=None, summary='In Texas, certain personal property is exempt from seizure by creditors up to an aggregate value of $100,000 for a family or $50,000 for a single adult. **Per-model derivation results:**
  - claude-opus-5: grounded=True, error=None, summary="Under Craddock, a default judgment 'should be set aside and a new trial ordered in any case in which' three conditions are met: (1) the defendant's failure to answer was not intentional or the result of conscious indifference, but due to mistake or accident; (2) the motion sets up a meritorious defense; and (3) it is filed at a time when granting it will cause no delay or other injury to the plaintiff. Timing is fixed by rule: the motion must be filed within thirty days after the judgment is signed (Rule 329b(a)), and if no written order is signed within seventy-five days of the judgment, the motion is overruled by operation of law (Rule 329b(c))."

Proc. § 704.730(a), a judgment debtor's homestead exemption equals the greater of (1) the countywide median sale price for a single-family home in the calendar year before the year the exemption is claimed, capped at $600,000, or (2) a $300,000 floor. So the exemption is never less than $300,000 and never more than $600,000 under the baseline figures. Subdivision (b) provides that both figures adjust annually for inflation starting January 1, 2022, using the change in the annual California Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers published by the Department of Industrial Relations for the most recent one-year period ending December 31 before the adjustment, rounded to the nearest $25."

  - gemini-2.5-pro: grounded=True, error=None, summary="The California homestead exemption is the greater of two amounts: either the county's median sale price for a single-family home from the prior year (up to a maximum of $600,000), or a baseline of $300,000. The applicable wage is the state minimum hourly wage in effect when the earnings are payable, or the local minimum wage if the debtor works where the local minimum is higher. This version of the section became operative September 1, 2023. Note: the quoted text states a general garnishment cap and does not itself contain a separate rule specific to consumer-debt judgments, which could appear elsewhere in the chapter under the 'except as otherwise provided' clause."
  - gpt-5.5: grounded=True, error=None, summary='As of September 1, 2023, the maximum disposable earnings of an individual judgment debtor subject to an earnings withholding order for a workweek is the lesser of: 20% of that week’s disposable earnings, or 40% of the amount by which that week’s disposable earnings exceed 48 times the applicable minimum hourly wage. The state minimum wage is used unless the debtor works where the local minimum wage is higher, in which case the local minimum wage is used.'
  - gemini-2.5-pro: grounded=True, error=None, summary="As of September 1, 2023, the maximum amount of an individual's weekly disposable earnings that can be garnished is the lesser of two figures. A timely written dispute or original-creditor request triggers § 1692g(b): the collector must cease collection of the disputed portion until verification, a judgment copy, or the original creditor's name and address is mailed to the consumer; otherwise collection may continue during the 30 days. **Per-model derivation results:**
  - claude-opus-5: grounded=True, error=None, summary="Section 1692f imposes a general ban on a debt collector's use of unfair or unconscionable means to collect a debt, then lists non-exhaustive examples of violations. These include collecting any amount (interest, fees, charges, expenses) not expressly authorized by the debt agreement or permitted by law; accepting a check postdated more than five days without written notice of intent to deposit given 3-10 business days beforehand; soliciting postdated instruments to threaten or institute criminal prosecution; depositing or threatening to deposit a postdated instrument early; causing a person to incur communication charges by concealing the communication's true purpose; and taking or threatening nonjudicial dispossession or disablement of property where there is no present enforceable right to possession, no present intent to take possession, or the property is exempt by law. It also bars communicating with a consumer about a debt by post card and using any language or symbol on a mailed envelope other than the collector's address, though a business name may be used if it does not indicate the debt collection business."
  - gpt-5.5: grounded=True, error=None, summary='A debt collector may not use unfair or unconscionable means to collect or attempt to collect any debt. The listed violations include collecting unauthorized amounts; certain improper handling or solicitation of postdated checks; causing communication charges by concealing the purpose of a communication; improper nonjudicial repossession or disablement actions; communicating about a debt by postcard; and using prohibited language or symbols on mailed envelopes or telegrams.'
  - gemini-2.5-pro: grounded=True, error=None, summary='A debt collector may not use unfair or unconscionable means to collect a debt, which includes a number of specific, prohibited actions. These forbidden acts include collecting any amount not authorized by the agreement or law, misusing postdated checks, and taking or threatening to take property without a present right to do so. Other prohibited practices are communicating about a debt via postcard and using any language or symbol on an envelope, other than an address, that indicates the communication is from a debt collector.'

The statute lists prohibited conduct including false claims about government affiliation, the debt’s character/amount/legal status, attorney involvement, legal consequences of nonpayment, threats of actions that are illegal or unintended, false credit reporting, simulated official documents, deceptive collection means, false legal-process statements, use of a non-true business name, and false claims of affiliation with a consumer reporting agency. Debt collectors also must give the required debt-collection disclosure in initial and subsequent consumer communications, with an exception for formal pleadings in legal actions.'

Collectors must also disclose in their initial communication that they are attempting to collect a debt and, in subsequent communications, that the message is from a debt collector, unless it is a formal legal pleading."

**Per-model derivation results:**
  - claude-opus-5: grounded=True, error=None, summary="Under 12 C.F.R. § 1006.14(b)(2)(i), a debt collector is presumed to comply with the harassment prohibition in § 1006.14(b)(1) and FDCPA § 806(5) if it places calls to a particular person about a particular debt neither more than seven times in seven consecutive days, nor within seven consecutive days after having had a telephone conversation with that person about that debt. Paragraph (b)(2)(ii) flips the presumption: exceeding either of those two frequencies creates a presumption that the collector violated (b)(1) and FDCPA § 806(5). The limits are applied per 'particular debt,' meaning each individual debt in collection, except that multiple student loan debts serviced under a single account number when obtained by the collector count as one particular debt (§ 1006.14(b)(4))."
  - gpt-5.5: grounded=True, error=None, summary='A debt collector is presumed to comply if, for a particular person and a particular debt, it does not place more than seven calls within seven consecutive days and does not call within seven consecutive days after having a telephone conversation with that person about that debt. A debt collector is presumed to violate the rule if it exceeds either of those call-frequency limits. For this purpose, a “particular debt” generally means each debt in collection, with a special rule grouping certain student loan debts serviced under one account number.'
  - gemini-2.5-pro: grounded=True, error=None, summary="A debt collector is presumed to have violated the law if they place a telephone call to a person about a particular debt more than seven times within seven consecutive days. This presumption also applies if the collector calls within seven consecutive days after having a telephone conversation with that person regarding the debt. For this rule, each debt is generally treated separately, but all student loans serviced under a single account number are treated as a single 'particular debt'."

  - claude-opus-5: grounded=True, error=None, summary="Once a furnisher receives notice from a consumer reporting agency under § 1681i(a)(2) that a consumer disputes the completeness or accuracy of information it supplied, the furnisher must investigate the disputed information, review all relevant information the agency provided with the notice, and report the investigation's results back to that agency. If the investigation shows the information is incomplete or inaccurate, the furnisher must also report those results to all other nationwide consumer reporting agencies it furnished the information to, and must promptly modify, delete, or permanently block reporting of any item found inaccurate, incomplete, or unverifiable. All of these investigations, reviews, and reports must be completed within the same time period § 1681i(a)(1) gives the credit bureau to complete its reinvestigation."
  - gpt-5.5: grounded=True, error=None, summary='After receiving notice from a consumer reporting agency that a consumer disputes the completeness or accuracy of information the person furnished, the furnisher must investigate the disputed information, review all relevant information provided by the agency, and report the investigation results back to the agency. If the information is found incomplete, inaccurate, or cannot be verified, the furnisher must take appropriate reporting action by modifying, deleting, or permanently blocking the item, and in some cases report results to other nationwide consumer reporting agencies to which it furnished the information. The furnisher must complete the required investigations, reviews, and reports within the time period that applies to the consumer reporting agency’s required actions under section 1681i(a)(1).'

For stated or open accounts, the statute adds that no item of the account is barred so long as any item on it was incurred within the three years immediately before the action is brought. The three-year period also covers claims for relief based on fraud or mistake, which do not accrue until the aggrieved party discovers the facts constituting the fraud or mistake; mutual and current merchant-to-merchant trade accounts are excluded from the open-account category.'

Actions on stated or open accounts are also subject to a three-year period, and no item of a stated or open account is barred if any item was incurred within the three years immediately before the action is brought.'
  - gemini-2.5-pro: grounded=True, error=None, summary='According to the provided text, a lawsuit must be started within three years after the cause of action accrues for a debt that is not based on a written contract. The dispute window runs 30 days from the consumer's receipt (or assumed receipt) of that information, and § 1006.34(b)(5) lets the collector assume receipt on any date at least five business days (excluding weekends and legal public holidays) after it provides the information, so the consumer is still timely if the written dispute is sent before that 30-day period ends. A timely written dispute (or original-creditor request) under § 1692g(b) obliges the collector to cease collection of the disputed portion until verification, a judgment copy, or the original creditor's name and address is mailed; otherwise collection may continue during the 30 days. The excerpt supplies only this standard — applying it to a particular consumer requires the actual notice content and the sending/receipt dates, which the text does not provide."
